#2894. Deere (DE) Q4 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Management says the ag cycle bottomed in 2026 as replacement demand builds.

By Motley Fool Transcribing – Nov 25, 2026 at 3:23PM EST

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Deere (DE +4.59%) reported full-year net income of $4.86 billion and confirmed that Equipment Operations delivered a 12.2% operating margin despite varied market conditions. Management reported that the agricultural equipment cycle reached its trough in 2026, with North America leading a measured recovery driven by replacement demand for an aging machine fleet. The company's fiscal year 2027 outlook anticipates net income between $4.5 billion and $5.25 billion, a range that accounts for a one-time non-cash pension settlement charge and an estimated $300 million net tariff headwind. Management stated that structurally higher margins and diversified earnings across construction, dairy, and livestock markets provided stability during the downturn in large agriculture. The company plans to increase capital investments in technology and automation while maintaining flexible production schedules to calibrate inventory levels based on end-market demand developments.

Operator: Good morning, and welcome to Deere & Company Fourth Quarter Earnings Conference Call. Your lines have been placed on listen only until the question-and-answer session of today's conference. I would now like to turn the call over to Christopher (Chris) Seibert, Director of Investor Relations. Thank you. You may begin.

Christopher (Chris) Seibert: Hello. Welcome, and thank you for joining us on today's call. And a happy early Thanksgiving for those of you celebrating tomorrow. Joining me on the call today are John C. May, Chairman and Chief Executive Officer, T. Brent Norwood, Chief Financial Officer, and Dan Pulley, Manager Investor Communications. Today, we'll take a closer look at Deere's fourth quarter earnings and spend some time talking about our end markets and our current outlook for fiscal 2027. After that, we'll respond to your questions. Please note that slides are available to complement the call this morning. They can be accessed on our website at JohnDeere.com/earnings.

First, a reminder, this call is broadcast live on the internet and recorded for future transmission and use by Deere & Company. Any other use, recording, or transmission of any portion of this copyrighted broadcast without the express written consent of Deere is strictly prohibited. Participants in the call, including the Q&A session, agree that their likeness and remarks in all media may be stored and used as part of the earnings call. This call includes forward-looking statements concerning the company's plans and projections for the future that are subject to uncertainties, risks, changes in circumstances, and other factors that are difficult to predict.

Additional information concerning factors that could cause actual results to differ materially is contained in the company's most recent Form 8-K, risk factors in the annual Form 10-K, as updated by reports filed with the Securities and Exchange Commission. This call also may include financial measures that are not in conformance with accounting principles generally accepted in the U.S., GAAP. Additional information concerning these measures, including reconciliations to comparable GAAP measures, is included in the release and posted on our website at JohnDeere.com/earnings under Quarterly Earnings and Events. I will now turn the call over to Dan Pulley.

Dan Pulley: Thank you, Chris, and good morning. Deere & Company closed out fiscal 2026 with a strong fourth quarter, delivering 9.3% margins within Equipment Operations. For the full year, operating margins for the Equipment Operations came in at 12.2%, resulting in $4.86 billion of net income. Market conditions varied considerably across our businesses this year. In row crop agriculture, our customers managed through a challenging farm economy, while end markets across construction, compact construction, and Small Ag & Turf remained resilient, supported by healthy project activity and steady product demand. Throughout 2026, teams across our factories, warehouses, and offices executed effectively and managed production changes without disruption in a dynamic global market environment.

At the same time, we continued investing in solutions our customers are putting to work across their farms and job sites to help them lower costs, boost productivity, and improve operating outcomes. Looking ahead to 2027, North America is leading the measured recovery in agriculture, with our Early Order Programs pointing to improving demand as an aged fleet and tight field inventories drive replacement needs. In construction, demand for North American earthmoving equipment remains strong, supported in part by data center investment. We will cover more details later in the call, including what is reflected in our wider guidance ranges. Let us now review the results of fiscal year 2026, beginning on Slide three.

Net sales and revenues were up 4% to $47.7 billion, while net sales for the Equipment Operations were up 6% to $41.3 billion. Net income attributable to Deere & Company was $4.86 billion, or $17.95 per diluted share. Moving now to Slide four and our results for the fourth quarter. Net sales and revenues were down 2% to $12.1 billion, and net sales for the Equipment Operations were down 1% to $10.5 billion. Net income attributable to Deere & Company for the quarter was down 1% to $1.05 billion, or $3.89 per diluted share. Let us now take a closer look at fourth quarter results by segment, beginning with our Production & Precision Ag business on Slide five.

Net sales of $4 billion were down 15% compared to the fourth quarter last year, primarily due to lower shipments. Price realization in the quarter was positive by half a point. Currency translation was also positive by about one point. Operating profit was $406 million, resulting in a 10.1% operating margin for the segment. The year-over-year decrease in operating profit was primarily due to lower shipment volumes and sales mix, partially offset by lower production costs and the effects of foreign currency exchange. Turning to Small Ag & Turf on Slide six. Net sales were up 10% year-over-year, totaling $2.7 billion in the fourth quarter, primarily due to higher shipment volumes and favorable price realization.

Price realization in the quarter was positive by approximately 1.5 Points. Currency translation for the quarter was unfavorable by roughly one point. Operating profit for the quarter increased year-over-year to $233 million, primarily due to higher shipment volumes and sales mix, lower warranty expenses, lower production costs, and favorable price realization. Slide seven details our fiscal year 2027 Ag & Turf industry outlook. For Large Ag in the U.S. and Canada, we expect industry sales to be up 5%-15%. The improvement in crop prices during August and September gave many growers an opportunity to market a portion of their crop at improved profitability.

Used inventories have also continued to decline, particularly for combines, leaving dealers in the market better positioned as we enter 2027. At the same time, crop prices have pulled back from their recent highs, and with input costs elevated, farm margins remain tight, which will likely moderate the pace of industry improvement. Even so, customer orders across our North American Early Order Programs are running up double digits from last year, reflecting resilient replacement demand from an aging fleet as customers look for greater productivity. Small Ag & Turf's diverse end markets continue to provide stability for the business. For 2027, we expect industry sales in the U.S. and Canada to be about flat.

Dairy and livestock fundamentals remain healthy, supported by stable, strong cattle prices. Within the turf equipment industry, high mortgage rates and a soft housing market are limiting growth, but low unemployment and strong household net worth continue to support a stable demand outlook as we enter 2027. In Europe, we expect industry sales to be flat to down 5%. While dairy and livestock fundamentals remain favorable, lower crop yields due to recent drought conditions have pressured grower cash flows. When combined with elevated input costs and tighter lending conditions, these pressures continue to delay investment decisions. As a result, we expect industry conditions to remain subdued through 2027.

Within South America, we expect Ag industry sales to be flat to up 5%, keeping industry volumes near historic lows. Conditions across the region remain challenging, particularly in Brazil, where high interest rates and recent currency volatility continue to weigh on purchasing decisions. Weather is also a key variable this season. Soybean planting is running ahead of last year's pace, but a strong El Niño is adding uncertainty as the crop develops. At the same time, a weaker currency is supporting local cash crop receipts.

We expect the industry backdrop to remain challenging early in the year, but we're monitoring the market closely as a rapidly aging fleet and low field inventory levels could help drive a recovery in demand in the back half of the year. In Asia, we expect industry sales to be roughly flat, with slight gains across most of the region offsetting a moderation in India after a strong 2026. Turning to our segment outlooks, beginning on Slide eight. For fiscal year 2027, we expect Production & Precision Ag net sales to increase 5%-15%. This includes approximately 3 points of price realization, partially offset by 1.5 points of unfavorable currency impact year-over-year.

The wider range balances growing confidence in North America with continued uncertainty in other key regions. We are projecting a full-year operating margin of 12.5%-14.5% for the segment, driven by higher sales volumes in North America and favorable price realization, partially offset by production cost inflation. Next, Slide nine covers Small Ag & Turf outlook. Net sales for fiscal year 2027 are expected to be about flat, including approximately 2.5 points of price realization and about 1 point of unfavorable currency impact. Operating margin for the segment is forecast to be in the range of 12.5%-13.5%, reflecting a narrower sales outlook supported by diversified end markets and favorable price realization, balanced by increased investments to support our growth initiatives.

Moving to Construction & Forestry on Slide 10. Net sales for the fourth quarter were up 12% year-over-year to $3.8 billion, primarily due to higher shipment volumes and favorable price realization. Price realization in the quarter was positive by approximately 4 points. Currency translation for the quarter was roughly flat. Operating profit was slightly down year-over-year at $339 million, resulting in an 8.9% operating margin. The change in operating profit was primarily due to increased production costs and higher SA&G and R&D costs, partially offset by favorable price realization. Slide 11 outlines our 2027 Construction & Forestry industry outlook. In the U.S. and Canada, we expect industry sales for both construction and compact construction to be flat to up 5%.

Elevated infrastructure spending and strong non-residential construction, partly driven by data center projects, continue to support healthy contractor backlogs. At the same time, rental companies are refreshing their fleets as rental rates and utilization improve, and used equipment inventories have tightened. These tailwinds are partly offset by continued softness in residential construction as elevated mortgage rates weigh on housing activity. We are also watching the pace of data center investment closely, as any slowdown could temper the current momentum. Within road building, we expect industry sales to be flat to up 5%, supported globally by infrastructure programs and major projects, most notably in North America. Global forestry markets are expected to be roughly flat.

Industry activity should stabilize after a difficult 2026, but the continuation of a soft housing market will limit improvement. Continuing with C&F, our fiscal year 2027 financial outlook for this segment is provided on Slide 12. We expect fiscal year 2027 net sales to range from flat to up 10%, with approximately 2 points of price realization and 1 point of unfavorable currency impact. We anticipate the segment's operating margin to be between 9.5% and 11.5%, as continued strength within the construction industry reinforces incremental investments to unlock growth. Moving to Financial Services on Slide 13. Worldwide Financial Services net income attributable to Deere & Company was $231 million in the fourth quarter.

The year-over-year decrease was driven by unfavorable financing spreads, the impact of a lower average portfolio, and a higher provision for credit losses. For the full year, Financial Services net income was $885 million. For fiscal year 2027, we expect net income of $900 million. The increase is primarily driven by improved financing spreads and a lower provision for credit losses as customer credit conditions continue to stabilize. Slide 14 concludes with our fiscal year 2027 guidance for net income, effective tax rate, and net operating cash flow. For fiscal year 2027, we expect net income between $4.5 billion and $5.25 billion.

This outlook includes roughly $500 million in higher pension-related impacts compared with last year, most of which relates to an estimated one-time tax-adjusted non-cash pension settlement charge of $250 million-$400 million associated with a voluntary lump sum payment option offered to certain U.S. salaried retirees. At this time, we expect to incur this one-time non-cash charge in the second quarter. Our effective tax rate for fiscal year 2027 is expected to be between 23% and 25%. Lastly, net operating cash flow is projected to be in the range of $4.5 billion-$5.5 billion. This concludes our formal remarks. Before we turn to a few key topics, I would like to ask John and Brent to share some opening remarks.

John C. May: Thanks, Dan. This is John. I want to start by thanking our employees for their focus and dedication this year, our dealers for working with us to manage inventory and support the business, and our customers for the trust they place in John Deere. I am proud of what we accomplished together this past year. Fiscal 2026 brought varied market conditions, with each of our businesses at a different point in the cycle. In Large Ag, farmers are still working through tight margins, increasing input costs, and volatile commodity markets. In Construction, markets kept getting stronger, with infrastructure and rental fleet investments supporting strong order books.

In Small Ag & Turf, a diverse mix of end markets, from dairy and livestock to turf, gave a supportive backdrop for growth. Through it all, our teams kept inventories healthy, stayed focused on the investments that mattered most for our customers, and delivered double-digit margins in every segment. For me, that's what stands out most about this year. It showed the strength of Smart Industrial across our business units. Our strategy is to keep investing and innovating through the cycle. That consistency is what makes our growth ambitions possible. In 2026, we showed that we mean it.

Our structurally higher margins and consistent cash generation gave us the flexibility to keep investing in the business while also pursuing targeted acquisitions. The way our team executed this year leaves us well-positioned for 2027, even as our wider guide ranges reflect a broader set of possible outcomes across the industries we serve. Looking ahead to 2027, I have two important takeaways I want to share with you. First, the Ag Equipment cycle troughed in 2026. The proof points from our Early Order Programs point to North America leading the global recovery. External factors like geopolitical influences on crop input inflation will likely moderate the pace of that recovery in the near term.

Even so, an aged fleet and limited field inventories for new equipment are driving required replacement demand, even as farm economics remain tight. We do see further upside for the year, which is why our sales range is wider. The upside depends on filling out the remainder of our North American order book at the pace similar to our Early Order Programs. The other option that could happen is on a backend-loaded recovery in Brazil, where aging fleet dynamics are building rapidly due to a machine usage intensity that is twice the level of North America. Second, momentum remains very strong in North American earthmoving products.

Much of the demand relies on growth stemming from continued data center investment, so we're closely watching current developments around prospective data center assessments from local communities. We've left enough flexibility in our build schedules to calibrate field inventory levels as needed. Brent, can you expand on the outlook for next year?

T. Brent Norwood: Sure, John. For 2027, we are encouraged by the early improvement we are seeing in the North American Large Ag order books, though uncertainty remains as we navigate end market complexities and a range of potential outcomes across several key regions. Our wider sales guidance ranges for both PPA and C&F reflect those complexities. In Production & Precision Ag, we expect net sales up between 5%-15%. This range reflects visible improvements to the North American Ag Equipment industry, though the ultimate magnitude of that improvement will depend on a number of factors, most of which have been volatile as of late.

Aging fleets and required replacement demand, coupled with declining used inventories, give the lower end of our sales outlook a very solid foundation. The upper end of our guidance range reflects a sustained finish to our combine Early Order Program and improving customer confidence throughout the year that carries into our rolling tractor order book. Conditions in Europe and in Brazil remain difficult, and there's greater uncertainty around when those two markets will turn. We believe Brazil could improve in 2027, given the current industry volumes at historic lows, combined with a rapidly aging fleet. At the same time, crop fundamentals, weather risk, and high interest rates will likely keep any improvement muted until the back half of 2027.

Moving to Construction & Forestry, we expect sales flat to up 10%. Construction end markets remain healthy, and our order books are strong, giving us good visibility into next year. The duration of demand tailwinds from data centers and other large non-residential projects is something we're watching closely, though most of that uncertainty is weighted towards 2028. Currently, dealer fleets remain below target levels, following a couple years of cumulative underproduction. This gives us room to improve those positions and build field inventory should confidence in end market outlooks continue to build. Our sales guide reflects strong near-term demand while maintaining flexibility on field inventory positioning as we monitor how demand develops heading into 2028.

Lastly, Small Ag & Turf is more balanced, with its diverse end markets and global footprint providing solid footing for next year. While we anticipate some downward industry pressure in Europe, we expect sales to be roughly flat, supported by continued strength across dairy, livestock, and turf markets.

Dan Pulley: Thank you both. Now we will cover a few topics before opening the line for questions. Let's start with our 2026 results. Chris, can you walk us through the fourth quarter, including how we finished the year relative to our guidance?

Christopher (Chris) Seibert: Sure, Dan. Happy to do that. The fourth quarter was a good example of the balance across our portfolio. Sales for Production & Precision Ag were down 15% on the quarter, as North American Large Ag demand stayed at low levels, and we kept production disciplined in South America. Conversely, Small Ag & Turf and Construction & Forestry grew 10% and 12% respectively on higher shipments and favorable price realization. This positioned overall Equip Ops sales to finish roughly flat for the quarter, with operating margins of 9.3%, reflecting disciplined spend management and strong execution to close out the year. All segments were in line or ahead of the guidance we gave last quarter.

For the full year, C&F sales rose nearly 22%, slightly ahead of our full-year outlook, following a strong fourth quarter finish. Infrastructure and non-residential construction, along with rental fleet replacement and growth, supported demand throughout the year. The 10.6% operating margin landed within our guide range, as favorable volumes and price realization throughout the year were partly offset by higher production costs and increased investments within SA&G and R&D tied to acquisitions and growth initiatives. Small Ag & Turf grew sales by roughly 15%, in line with our outlook, and delivered a 15.1% operating margin, reflecting strength across all regions, including a record year for our tractor business in India.

In Production & Precision Ag, full-year sales were down about 9%, reflecting industry declines across North America, South America, and Europe. Even at those demand levels, the segment delivered an 11.3% operating margin as we maintained production discipline, worked alongside our dealers to keep new inventories tight, reduced used inventory, and continued investing in the technology solutions our customers rely on.

T. Brent Norwood: Thanks, Chris. This is Brent. I want to emphasize how the business has changed through the cycle. Despite our largest business, PPA, operating in its second year at sub-trough levels, our Equip Ops as a whole delivered margin of more than 12%. This is more than 400 basis points higher than in 2016, when we were at a comparable point in the industry cycle, and nearly 700 basis points higher if we exclude tariffs. That performance reflects the structural changes we have made across the business: better cost management, tighter inventory discipline, and a growing share of revenue tied to high-value technologies.

Just as important, our earnings are far more diversified than they were a decade ago, when North American Large Ag mostly determined our financial results. This year, Construction & Forestry and Small Ag & Turf both grew by double digits while delivering strong profitability. For Production & Precision Ag, every region delivered double-digit margins despite challenging market conditions. That balance across segments and regions provides greater earnings stability and gives us the flexibility to continue investing for our customers through the cycle.

Dan Pulley: Thanks, Brent and Chris. Before we shift gears to 2027, let's discuss dealer inventory levels. Chris, can you help us understand where those stand today for North American Large Ag?

Christopher (Chris) Seibert: Sure, Dan. Our inventory of new Large Ag machines remains well-positioned and tight, down 10% from already low levels last year, with very few large combines and high-horsepower tractors across dealer lots. As we discussed in past quarters, we've prioritized managing used inventory since the downturn began, and the results clearly demonstrate those efforts. In North America, used high-horsepower tractor and combine inventories are both down double digits from a year ago. High-horsepower tractors are now down 25% from their peak, and used combines are down roughly 30%. More importantly, the quality of that inventory has improved as well.

The model year distribution of used combines is in line with our targets, and the mix of tractors has improved significantly, with model year 2023 and 2024 inventory down more than 40% from last year. Specifically, within this past quarter, we saw very strong activity in used combines, as year-end purchasing and harvest activity drove a double-digit reduction in inventory across our North American dealer network. We'll be watching for similar year-end purchasing activity in used tractors, which would further improve the setup for new tractor sales heading into the next growing season.

Dan Pulley: Thanks, Chris, for those details and the setup for demand next year. Let's shift to our outlook for agriculture and our order book positions as we enter the year. Can you walk us through the different regions?

Christopher (Chris) Seibert: Yeah. Let me start in North America, where we're seeing encouraging signs. In large tractors, order backlog momentum for fiscal year 2027 has grown steadily in recent weeks, and we now have orders in hand through the middle of the second quarter. Spring use products finished their Early Order Programs up double digits overall, with particularly strong demand for planters. While the combine program is still underway, our orders are currently up double digits compared with the same point last year, and are already flat compared to the full prior year Early Order Program. Shifting to Europe, order activity moderated as we finished 2026, reflecting the challenging conditions.

Even so, our order books remain well-positioned for next year's outlook and currently extend into the second quarter. Within South America, the outlook for Ag markets remains mixed, but our first quarter order book in Brazil is full, and we just opened second quarter for orders recently. We're keeping production plans flexible within the region so we can respond quickly if conditions improve. Last, in Asia, we expect a roughly flat industry with improving end markets across much of the region offsetting moderation in India following a very strong 2026.

John C. May: This is John. We touched on the near-term uncertainty in South America, and I'd like to balance that with a longer-term view. Brazil remains one of the most important growth markets in agriculture, with expanding production and a customer base that is quickly adopting technology. As an example, this is the first year customers can order factory-installed See & Spray in Brazil. While it's still early, take rates are running at roughly twice, two times the level we saw during the first year in North America. We are also continuing to place JDLink Boost on every Large Ag machine leaving the factory to improve connectivity for our customers and heighten access to our technologies.

When the market turns, we'll be ready, and we remain excited about the long-term value we can create for growers across the region.

Dan Pulley: Thanks, John and Chris. Let's now turn to Construction & Forestry, which delivered strong growth in 2026. Chris, what are we seeing for order activity as we head into 2027?

Christopher (Chris) Seibert: Overall, demand remains healthy, and we're entering the year with good visibility. Our U.S. and Canadian order bank continued to strengthen through 2026, and we currently have demand visibility into the second quarter for both construction and compact construction equipment. The order pace continues to be driven by rental companies replenishing and expanding their fleets, and by large contractors with long project backlogs as infrastructure, pipeline, and data center work calls for larger fleets and more productive machines. That said, the demand strength isn't evenly spread. Backlogs for smaller and mid-sized contractors have tightened slightly as residential and private construction softens.

Overall, though, the order velocity supports our sales outlook, and we will also look to improve our dealer inventory positions as confidence in the longer-term end market demand improves. Moving to road building, we currently have roughly three to four months of order visibility, which is within our target range. Infrastructure demand continues to grow, particularly in North America, where public infrastructure programs and major projects support fleet growth. Finally, we expect forestry to be roughly flat, as soft housing markets limit a recovery, with order visibility also carrying into the second quarter at this time.

John C. May: This is John. Beyond the end market demand that Chris covered, what also excites me in construction is how quickly customers are adopting technology. Sales of our grade management, job site safety, and automation solutions grew roughly 30% in 2026. On our new 20 metric ton excavators, more than 90% of the orders include grade control. Contractors are dealing with the same labor constraints our farmers are, so they want technology that helps them work faster, safer, with less rework. We're also seeing rapid uptake of our fleet management job site solutions, which are on pace to exceed our expectations for growth.

Dan Pulley: Thanks, John. Let's continue with a technology topic and shift over to agriculture. Chris, how are customers using our precision ag solutions, and what are we seeing in adoption?

Christopher (Chris) Seibert: Even with a tough year in Ag markets, technology adoption kept building. Engaged Acres are now over 530 million globally. Highly Engaged Acres, where customers are recording multiple passes of activity and data on the same field, surpassed 200 million acres. Monthly active digital users, which measures the engagement with our digital solutions, were up nearly 25% to more than 500,000. And connected equipment grows at a similar pace to more than 1.2 million machines. Across our technologies, growing utilization is one of the clearest signs of the value customers are seeing. See & Spray is a good example.

Last year, we reported five million acres of utilization in the U.S. and Canada, and on that same basis, acres grew roughly 30% this year. However, globally, including Australia and Brazil, See & Spray covered approximately 12 million acres in 2026, delivering average herbicide savings of more than 50% across those acres. That momentum is continuing, with roughly one-third of orders in our recently concluded sprayer Early Order Program, including See & Spray. Moving to combines. Combines equipped with our Harvest Settings Automation technologies utilized the technology on more than double the acres this year, totaling to 11 million acres. And with Predictive Ground Speed Automation engaged, customers are achieving roughly 20% more productivity while also consuming less fuel.

As industry conditions improve and more customers put these solutions to work, the impact will only grow, creating an ever-expanding set of data that can unlock even greater insights for customers within Operations Center.

John C. May: This is John, and I want to build on Chris's point about the scale of our data. I'm really excited to share this new product with you because I think it's going to be a major unlock for our customers in agriculture. I want you to picture a single field. In Operations Center, a customer can see the seed variety and rate, how the planter and combine were set, what was sprayed and where, and how the weather played out that year. Now, multiply that opportunity across more than 500 million acres in Operations Center, season after season. That's the richest dataset in agriculture.

Beyond these agronomic layers, our approach to machine integration creates an open environment where customers can manage their entire fleet, regardless of brand, to see machine performance, maximize uptime, and drive productivity. This depth in data only works because farmers trust us with it, and we have spent more than 15 years earning the trust as stewards of their data. Farmers know how their data is used. They decide who it is shared with, and they can stop sharing at any time. That trust lets us take this to the next step. Introducing JD, the first AI assistant, is now in use around the world.

Growers can ask questions about their fields and machines in plain language and get answers drawn from their own operations history. In the first week of full availability, more than 6,000 users in 76 countries held more than 10,000 conversations with JD, and this is just the beginning. We will keep expanding JD to help our customers make faster, better, and more informed decisions.

Dan Pulley: Thank you, Chris and John. Before we wrap up, let us spend a few minutes on our outlook on costs. Chris, can you provide us with some details?

Christopher (Chris) Seibert: Yeah. Maybe first of all, cost discipline has always been part of how we run the business. Through this downturn, our teams did a great job of executing efficiently, effectively, and finding productivity gains across our operations and supplies, which is a big reason our margins held up as well as they did. Heading into 2027, we are staying focused on what we can control. Raw material and freight costs are still moving higher, but we feel well-positioned with pricing set to manage anticipated inflationary pressures. We are also working closely with our suppliers on sourcing and design changes that will take costs out without risking quality. Moving on to tariffs.

Direct tariff costs were a bit more than $1.1 billion in 2026 before refunds. For 2027, we expect the run rate to come down slightly to roughly $1 billion, but that leaves us a net headwind of roughly $300 million since we will not see a repeat of the 2026 tariff refunds.

T. Brent Norwood: This is Brent. I will build on Chris's comments with a few additional points. Inside our own walls, we continue concentrating our production in modern, high-use facilities while investing in automation and more efficient production. These investments improve factory throughput, give us more flexibility in how we source, and position us to be cost-competitive for our customers. As we look ahead to next year, one item worth flagging is that our labor agreements with the UAW expire at the end of the fiscal year. We value our longstanding relationship with our workforce and look forward to constructive negotiations.

During those negotiations, our number one priority remains serving our customers' needs, keeping our machines running in the field, and maintaining continuity for our dealers. As such, we have built significant optionality into our operations to better position us to respond to a variety of outcomes, including a work stoppage with our labor force. Those plans include keeping enough inventory to support sales in fiscal year 2028, while preparing to continue operations through a work stoppage if necessary. The impact of these actions are likely to result in a notable increase in our working capital in the back half of fiscal year 2027.

Dan Pulley: Thank you both. Brent, staying with you, would you please share some final remarks before we open the line for questions?

T. Brent Norwood: Sure, Dan. In C&F, alongside our continued excavator investments, we're expanding our digital construction platform centered on Tenna. Maintaining our commitment to an A rating, funding the operations for growth of the business, and returning excess cash to shareholders through dividends and share repurchases. #2893. Report: Steelers are expected to release Aaron Rodgers

It's to be expected, given that quarterback Aaron Rodgers has poured rocket fuel on the fire that Steelers started last night when the Steelers benched Rodgers.

Via Gerry Dulac of the Pittsburgh Post-Gazette, the Steelers are expected to release Rodgers. Dulac said the decision was "hastened" by Rodgers's comments on Pat McAfee's show.

If/when it happens, Rodgers will initially be subject to waivers. He could be claimed, in theory. (It's unlikely.) He then would be a free agent.

What comes next will be fascinating. The passion and intensity of Rodgers's remarks suggest he won't go quietly, and that he'll want to play for someone else in order to prove that, with a functional head coach, Rodgers can still win games.

Of course, someone else will have to be willing to welcome the Aaron Rodgers Experience to town, where anything the coach says or does can and will be used against him in a future episode of The Pat McAfee Show.

#2892. Anthropic’s Chip Choices Could Change the Nvidia Story. Here’s How.

Anthropic is spreading out its demand for AI accelerators across multiple companies.

By Chris Neiger – Nov 25, 2026 at 3:33PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic ›

Anthropic (ANT +0.87%) made headlines recently because of its blockbuster IPO, and investors are still trying to decide how the newly public artificial intelligence (AI) company will impact the broader AI market.

Some of the details Anthropic disclosed in its S-1 filing before going public shed light on its potential impact, especially on chip companies. Anthropic currently has deals with multiple chip companies to help provide the compute capacity it needs for its AI services.

Nvidia (NVDA -3.94%) is, unsurprisingly, a key supplier of chips to Anthropic, but so are Alphabet (GOOGL -2.11%) (GOOG -1.98%), Amazon (AMZN -2.98%), and Advanced Micro Devices (AMD -1.46%). Instead of tying itself to just one chip company, Anthropic has decided to spread out its chip vendors.

That could change Nvidia's growth narrative in the coming years. Here's how it's shifting and why Nvidia investors don't need to panic just yet.

Between cloud computing contracts and chip deals, Anthropic is on the hook for about $900 billion in spending over the next several years. That's a heck of a lot of money to spend, and Anthropic hopes it will accelerate its sales far beyond the company's current annual revenue run rate of $81 billion.

It's also far too much money to funnel toward just one or two AI infrastructure companies. To minimize its supply chain risks, ensure it has the most advanced technology, and negotiate better chip prices, Anthropic spreads its chip deals across four key players.

Here are Anthropic's biggest AI chip deals:

While Anthropic doesn't disclose all the details of each deal, including how much they might be worth, this data shows that Anthropic is clearly hedging its bets when it comes to buying AI chips.

Nvidia's GPUs are still among the most in-demand chips in the world, and they're very likely to remain so for years to come due to AI infrastructure spending. Nvidia had an estimated 80%-90% market share of AI accelerators last year and is estimated to have 75% this year.

That's both good and bad news for Nvidia. Having three-quarters of the AI accelerator market is huge, and it's part of the reason why Nvidia just reported another impressive quarter, with Q3 fiscal 2027 revenue rising 103% to $115.8 billion and earnings per share jumping 111% to $2.68.

But AMD's GPUs and custom silicon from Google, Broadcom, and Amazon are reducing Nvidia's dominance. Part of the reason is that custom chips (like the ones Anthropic buys from Nvidia rivals) are good at handling agentic AI tasks and are more efficient than the raw processing power from GPUs.

What's more, Nvidia's gross margins, while still high, are declining. The company said gross margins will be between 71% and 72% in the fourth quarter, down from nearly 75% in the third quarter. This downward pressure is mostly coming from rising memory costs right now, but intensifying chip competition could weigh on margins further in the coming years.

Still, while Nvidia shareholders should be aware of how rival chip companies could impact the company over time, Nvidia is still the leading AI accelerator company. Its recent revenue guidance for 75% growth in the fourth quarter -- up from its previous guidance of 70% -- should reassure investors that the company isn't getting sidelined anytime soon.

#2891. Coca-Cola Has Raised Its Dividend 64 Straight Years. Here's What $10,000 Will Pay You in 2027.

This Dividend King pays investors reliably, but its stock price has run ahead of the business.

By Neil Patel – Nov 25, 2026 at 3:47PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Coca-Cola ›

Investors can't help but think about the 2026 performance of a popular defensive name. The S&P 500, with heavy exposure to technology and artificial intelligence, is only up 6% this year (as of Nov. 23). Coca-Cola (KO +0.44%), on the other hand, has soared 37%.

The beverage stock isn't known for its growth potential, which has been a pleasant surprise in 2026. Instead, investors choose to buy and hold this industry-leading company because it's a safe choice that can generate a healthy dividend stream.

Coca-Cola has raised its dividend payout for a mind-blowing 64 straight years, making it a Dividend King, or a company that has raised its payout for 50 years or more. Purchasing $10,000 worth of its shares today will bring in this much passive income in 2027.

Coca-Cola's board of directors approved a roughly 4% dividend hike on Feb. 19. The quarterly payout climbed from $0.51 to $0.53 per share, or $2.12 annualized.

Today's annual payout is 51% higher than the $1.40 per share paid in 2016. That's about 4.2% annualized growth. It isn't spectacular. But it's consistent, surviving a pandemic and a bout of high inflation.

Here's the math investors care about. Buying $10,000 at the current share price of $95.88 gets you 104 shares. Based on today's payout, that position would generate more than $220 in dividend income in 2027. If the board delivers another raise in February, a near certain event, that figure will obviously be higher.

The dividend yield sits at 2.21%. The S&P 500 yields just 1.12%. Consequently, $10,000 in an S&P 500 index fund would produce only about $112 a year. Coca-Cola nearly doubles that.

There's a catch, though. Coca-Cola yielded 2.66% the day after its February raise. This stock's impressive rally this year has pushed the yield much lower. Income investors are now paying more for every dollar of dividends.

Nobody questions the quality of this business. Coca-Cola sells drinks in more than 200 countries and territories. Its flagship trademark is the world's most valuable food and beverage brand, according to Kantar.

That brand strength translates into durable pricing power. In the third quarter, net revenue grew 7% year over year to $13.4 billion. Unit case volume, a measure of how much product consumers bought, jumped 5%. Yet price/mix (higher prices plus a richer product mix) still added 1%. Coca-Cola sold more without sacrificing price.

Profitability improved. The comparable operating margin, which excludes one-time items, widened to 32.9% from 31.9%. Comparable earnings per share (EPS) rose 11% to $0.91, beating Wall Street's $0.88 estimate. Management now expects full-year comparable EPS growth of at least 11%.

Valuation is the problem. Coca-Cola trades at a price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of 28.3. The S&P 500's index's P/E multiple is 20.3. That's a 39% premium for a company that isn't built for speed.

Management's long-term targets call for 4% to 5% organic revenue growth (excluding currency and acquisitions). Sell-side analysts expect the top line to shrink in 2027.

This year's gain was less about profits. The stock's 37% surge far outpaces that 11% earnings outlook for the year. Investors simply paid more for each dollar of earnings, with the P/E ratio rising 23% in 2026. That can't continue indefinitely.

I wouldn't buy Coca-Cola at today's price. With the best outcome being mid- to high-single-digit growth ahead, the valuation leaves little room for upside. Patient investors who appreciate the dividend should wait for a cheaper entry point.

#2889. Qualys's 2027 Outlook: Achieving Federal Authorization Solidifies Public Sector Moat

This under-the-radar cybersecurity stock boasts a Superscore of 85 from our Hidden Gems Primary database, part of The Motley Fool's Moneyball Database system. Here's why.

By Robert Izquierdo – Updated Nov 25, 2026 at 3:53PM EST

When a large enterprise needs to lock down its entire digital footprint, it turns to Qualys (QLYS +2.24%). Picture a security officer managing thousands of servers, cloud containers, and remote endpoints, all of which represent a potential entry point for a breach. Qualys provides the unified platform that identifies those risks and automates the fix, turning a chaotic network into a defensible one. As of November 19, 2026, the stock trades at $192.51, having risen 39% over the past year.

Our proprietary Hidden Gems scoring system assigns Qualys an overall Superscore of 85 out of 100, placing it in the Strong category. The Superscore is an AI-powered score that evaluates a company's overall strength by combining financial performance, product market position, technological capabilities, leadership quality, and relative valuation. It represents the unification of all our scores into a single score for public companies, with five rating bands: Exceptional (90-100), Strong (75-89), Above Average (60-74), Average (40-59), and Cautious (0-39).

An 85 Superscore places the company in the Top ~4% of every company we score, which is ahead of roughly 96 out of every 100 companies we evaluate. This Superscore is one data-driven signal worth investigating, and this article pairs the reasons the score is high with the reasons it is not higher so you can weigh both sides before doing more work.

Qualys is a highly capital-efficient business, earning outsized profits on a small base of hard tangible assets. This efficiency allows the company to turn each point of revenue growth into strong returns, a trait the market often rewards with a premium valuation. While this efficiency is a clear advantage, it does not erase the risks posed by elevated valuation multiples and aggressive competition.

The Hidden Gems Superscore reflects The Motley Fool's proprietary AI-driven evaluation of a company across product, financial, leadership, and valuation pillars as of the article date and may change over time. Performance figures are point-in-time. Past performance does not guarantee future results.

You may want to keep researching before buying if...

The Superscore is one data-driven signal worth investigating, not a stand-alone buy recommendation, and you should weigh it against your own research, financial goals, and risk tolerance before acting.

Qualys is well positioned in the cybersecurity space. Organizations and government agencies are shifting to platforms that not only identify security vulnerabilities, but also fixes them. That is one of the company's key strengths, which has grown in importance now that hackers can employ artificial intelligence to quickly attack a system before an organization can respond. As a result, Qualys saw its federal government-related sales outperform expectations in Q3 as agencies consolidated spending onto its platform.

Moreover, while many software companies operate at a loss, Qualys is profitable with an impressive Q3 gross margin of 83%. It sports a fortress balance sheet, exiting the third quarter with total assets of $1.2 billion compared to total liabilities of $536.3 million, and of those liabilities, more than $425 million is deferred revenue, which will transition to sales after services are delivered.

Given the company's many strengths, Qualys is well positioned for years of growth ahead. Yet these factors have contributed to the stock's elevated valuation, with a trailing P/E ratio that's around a high point for the past year. This suggests now is not the best time to buy. Wait for the price to drop first before deciding to pick up shares.

#2888. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#2887. Qualcomm vs. Sandisk: Which Technology Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Sandisk's revenue surged over 270%, driven by demand for cloud memory, while Qualcomm is branching into automotive and satellite connectivity. Which chip stock holds up better?

By Brendan Coffey – Updated Nov 25, 2026 at 4:00PM EST

As mobile markets mature and artificial intelligence expands, investors are looking for the next big chip winner. Is Qualcomm (QCOM +2.24%) or Sandisk (SNDK -5.57%) the better choice for your 2027 portfolio?

Qualcomm provides the wireless connectivity and processors that power modern smartphones and connected vehicles. Sandisk specializes in the flash storage and memory solutions required for high-speed data processing in cloud environments. Both companies are essential to the global technology infrastructure, but they offer vastly different financial profiles for investors to consider.

As one of the most prominent semiconductor stocks, Qualcomm designs the high-performance computing and connectivity platforms found in premium handsets and industrial devices. The company relies on a small group of mobile handset customers, including Apple Inc. (AAPL +0.43%) and Samsung, for a significant portion of its revenue. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business, particularly as Apple develops its own internal modem technology. To offset this, Qualcomm is expanding into automotive and data center markets while partnering with Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +1.30%) to provide radio platforms for the Starlink Mobile 5G network.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 27, 2026, revenue reached about $43.1 billion, representing a decrease of approximately 2.6% compared to the previous twelve-month period. Despite the slight revenue dip, the company reported a net income of roughly $13.6 billion for that time frame. Net margin for the year was about 32%, showing the company's ability to maintain high profitability even during a period of slowing top-line growth.

The company maintains a solid balance sheet, with a debt-to-equity ratio of approximately 0.5x as of its September 2026 balance sheet. This ratio compares total debt to shareholder equity, indicating the company maintains a manageable level of borrowing. Its current ratio, which measures the ability to cover short-term debts with short-term assets, stands at nearly 1.9x. For the fiscal year ended Sept. 27, 2026, free cash flow was nearly $8.7 billion. Note that stock-based compensation (SBC) represented roughly 31% of operating cash flow, which inflates reported cash generation since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

Sandisk operates as a major force in the flash memory industry, serving the massive data center, PC, and mobile device markets. The company provides the hardware necessary for storing and moving the vast amounts of data used by modern artificial intelligence systems. Unlike many chip peers, its sales are highly diverse, with no single customer accounting for more than 10% of revenue in the most recent fiscal year. This broad base of OEM and channel customers helps reduce the impact of any single partnership ending.

In the 12 months ended Oct. 2, 2026, revenue reached about $29 billion, a significant growth of approximately 272% over the prior year. This rapid expansion was accompanied by a net income of roughly $19.4 billion. The surge in sales and earnings reflects the intense demand for high-capacity memory solutions in the cloud computing sector. The company achieved a net margin of nearly 67% during this period, highlighting the strong demand for its memory technology.

As of July 2026, the company reported a debt-to-equity ratio of 0.0x, indicating that total liabilities do not exceed shareholder equity and its reliance on debt is minimal. Its current ratio was nearly 2.3x, suggesting a comfortable cushion for meeting its short-term financial obligations. In the fiscal year ended July 3, 2026, free cash flow reached nearly $11.5 billion. This robust cash generation allows the company to reinvest heavily in manufacturing facilities and next-generation storage research.

Qualcomm faces significant risks from its revenue concentration among a small number of handset manufacturers. The company expects Apple will transition to its own internally developed modems in the near future, which could impact sales. Furthermore, heavy exposure to the Chinese market makes the company vulnerable to shifting trade policies and national security regulations. Its reliance on a fabless model also means it depends on third-party foundries like Taiwan Semiconductor and Samsung for leading-edge production capacity.

Sandisk carries operational risks due to its heavy reliance on joint ventures with Kioxia for memory production. Its manufacturing facilities are geographically concentrated in Japan and Malaysia, leaving the supply chain open to disruption from natural disasters or geopolitical conflict. The memory industry is also notoriously cyclical, with intense competition from Nvidia Corp. (NVDA -3.91%), Western Digital (WDC -0.55%), and Micron Technology (MU -4.53%). The company must also manage the complexities associated with its separation from Western Digital, including potential tax liabilities.

Sandisk appears significantly cheaper based on future earnings estimates, while Qualcomm maintains a slightly higher premium for its diversified connectivity portfolio.

Valuation metrics include those sourced from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from those of other data providers.

Qualcomm's mobile phone business has been hammered this year, as weak Chinese demand and AI-driven memory shortages have caused handset-derived sales to fall below prior-year levels. Yet the AI boom has an upside for Qualcomm.

At the company's June 2026 investor day, Qualcomm raised its non-handset revenue target to more than $40 billion by 2029, nearly double the prior target of $22 billion. Qualcomm is reportedly partnering with OpenAI to develop smartphone processors with advanced AI capabilities. Analysts see this partnership as a positive for Qualcomm since it would enable advanced agentic AI on smartphone platforms. It would also diminish the business risk of Apple developing its own chips and dropping Qualcomm as a supplier.

Meanwhile, Sandisk shares have undergone remarkable appreciation since its spin-off from Western Digital created the firm as a stand-alone entity in February 2025. Shares went from $49 to more than $2,300 by the middle of 2026. Shares have since undergone an 8-for-1 split and seen a significant retracement of gains.

But the share strength shows just how strong the flash memory market has been for Sandisk. During periods of tight supply and strong market pricing, the company's profit margins can be robust, as they have been during this AI-driven market. Historically, though, high memory prices and low supply are followed by periods of high supply and low prices, which are a risk to Sandisk investors in the long run.

Still, the growth is impressive. Revenue should get near $50 billion 2027, more than double 2026, with Wall Street seeing more than $60 billion in sales in 2028. Net income should triple in 2027, to $34.9 billion, and grow more modestly in 2028.

The choice here is simply: Sandisk is booming and comes at a bargain-basement valuation while Qualcomm's expected improvement is still speculative at the moment. There is risk to Sandisk that one may be buying at the peak of the memory cycle, but it's clearly the better bet for 2027.

#2886. The Best Dividend Stocks to Buy With $5,000 in November

Here's a reliable high-yield stock, a dividend growth stock, and a turnaround story for you to consider.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 25, 2026 at 4:01PM EST

November is drawing to a close, but you still have some time to put money to work in dividend stocks. Say, $5,000, which could give you a toehold in a reliable high-yield stock like Federal Realty (FRT +1.50%), a dividend growth stock like Visa (V +1.98%), or a turnaround story like Pfizer (PFE +0.16%). Here's a look at each one and why, depending on how you prefer to invest, it could be a good place for your cash before the month is over.

Federal Realty is a bit unique in its strip-mall property niche, owning only around 100 assets. It is focused on being a sharpshooter, and its aim is very good. The proof of that is that it is the only Dividend King real estate investment trust (REIT). You don't string together 50+ annual dividend increases by accident; it requires a strong business model that is executed well in both good times and bad.

Federal Realty's approach is actually pretty simple. It buys properties surrounded by lots of wealthy customers. Then it redevelops the assets to ensure they are attractive to both consumers and tenants, including carefully curating the tenant list. When a property reaches full value, the REIT sells it and starts again with a new asset. That approach isn't particularly shocking, but it is hard to execute well. Federal Realty's dividend track record shows it consistently executes well.

You can find higher-yielding REITs pretty easily, but there's only one REIT that can claim to be a Dividend King. For conservative dividend investors, Federal Realty's well-above-market 4.2% yield should help you sleep well at night, even when the market gets turbulent. A $5,000 investment will let you buy 45 shares.

Visa's tiny 0.75% yield probably won't interest you if you are looking for high-yield stocks. That said, the dividend has grown by more than 370% over the past decade, which is a shockingly large number. If you don't need the income today and can set this stock to dividend reinvest, that kind of dividend growth could easily leave you with a robust income stream when you retire.

Visa is one of the world's largest payment processors. It has long benefited from the ongoing shift from paper currency to cards. It only collects a small percentage of each transaction it processes, but when a company handles 74.2 billion transactions in a single quarter (up 10% year over year), the numbers start to add up. And since the world is increasingly moving toward digital transactions, where paper money isn't an option, Visa is likely to have years of growth ahead. The stock isn't cheap, but given the growth opportunity here, it is probably still worth a look for long-term investors. A $5k investment will let you buy 12 shares.

Pfizer, an iconic drug maker, has a huge 6.2% yield. The average yield on pharma stocks is just 1.3%. The core problem Pfizer is facing is major patent expirations in the next few years, with Eliquis losing protection in 2028 and Vyndamax in 2031. It is a very real problem, but Pfizer is actively working on its pipeline to offset the revenue hit. Only, drug development doesn't work on a schedule, even though patent expirations do.

Investors clearly fear the worst, which could include a dividend cut. However, Pfizer's cash flow still covers its dividend, and it has over $14 billion of cash on its balance sheet. Moreover, the company continues to stand behind not only supporting the dividend at its current level, but growing it over time. Given the company's long history of success in the pharma sector, turnaround investors should give it a look. And even if there were a 50% dividend cut, which is probably a realistic worst-case scenario, the yield would still be over 3%. Five thousand dollars will buy you 178 shares.

No two investors are alike, which is why Wall Street is such an exciting place. When it comes to dividends, you can find boring and reliable dividend stocks like Federal Realty. You can find dividend growth stocks, such as Visa. And you can find turnaround stocks like Pfizer that come with ultra-high yields. Each one is worth a deep dive today.

#2885. The Dividend ETF I'd Choose to to Generate $500 a Month in Passive Income

It checks off a few boxes that make it a great fit for long-term investors.

By Stefon Walters – Nov 25, 2026 at 4:15PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Schwab U.S. Dividend Equity ETF ›

An extra $500 monthly has a nice ring to it. It could cover a couple of bills, a few grocery store runs, or simply sit in your savings for a rainy day. Either way, bringing in $500 monthly with no extra work is hard to beat.

There are plenty of dividend stocks and exchange-traded funds (ETFs) to choose from, but I'm a fan of the Schwab U.S. Dividend Equity ETF (SCHD +1.36%) because it's a trifecta: diversified, high-yield, and cheap. It's a dividend ETF you can trust and treat as a set-it-and-forget-it investment.

SCHD only holds 102 stocks, but they're well-established and vetted for quality. To be included in the fund, a company needs to be in good financial standing, have paid dividends for at least 10 years, and have increased its annual dividend for at least five years. Of course, some of its holdings can hit rough patches (as every company does), but those that check those boxes are usually built to last.

SCHD's holdings include 11 Dividend Kings (companies with at least 50 consecutive years of dividend increases), including four in its top 10 holdings: Procter & Gamble, Coca-Cola, PepsiCo, and Abbott Laboratories. Together, these four account for over 17% of the ETF, and Dividend Kings account for over 27% (as of Nov. 23).

SCHD can be a good complement to investing in the S&P 500 because it's not as tech-heavy as the index or many other popular indexes and ETFs. Most of its holdings come from more "boring" sectors like consumer staples (19.97%), healthcare (19.20%), and energy (15.38%).

Dividend yields move in the opposite direction of stock prices, so SCHD's 25% surge this year (as of Nov. 23) has pushed its yield down a bit. At the time of writing, its yield is 3.1%, which is below its average over the past three years, but it's still one of the highest yields you'll find in a major dividend ETF or blue chip dividend stocks.

Since SCHD is an ETF, its quarterly payouts aren't as straightforward as individual stocks. When Coca-Cola, for example, sets an annual dividend of $2.12, you know you'll get four $0.53 payouts. SCHD's holdings pay dividends at different times, so SCHD's payout fluctuates. But based on yields, here is how much you'd need invested to generate $500 monthly ($6,000 annually):

Generating $500 monthly from SCHD won't happen overnight for most investors, but it's an ETF you can comfortably add to over time and trust to compound toward your target. Ideally, you'd reinvest your dividends to buy more SCHD shares and speed up the process. The earlier you start, the better.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

#2880. Game Preview: San Antonio Spurs vs Portland Trail Blazers

After being stopped cold at 12 wins during their 1st win streak of the season, the Spurs have promptly started up another one, chugging right along even in the (one game) absence of their resident superstar.

Whether or not it continues will be decided by a challenging little three-game stretch against playoff teams, starting with last postseason's 1st round matchup, the Portland Trail Blazers.

The Spurs erupted from long-range against Portland in their first matchup this season, but past experience tells that these Blazers are far from pushovers, and are deeper than they were when they were giving the Spurs some degree of trouble in the playoffs. (Or, at least, were, until the injury bug bit them)

It seems unlikely that a Portland team ranking 11th in three-point percentage and 2nd in made threes will shoot 26% from downtown again, and the fact that this is an NBA Cup group stage may add a little zest to the quality of regular season effort in this one.

Still, the Spurs are 9-1 in their last 10 games, and if the Trail Blazers get absolutely manhandled on the boards again, they might not be able to get up enough shots anyway, having fallen eight threes shy of their season average as the Spurs held a 13 rebound advantage on the evening (6 of which were offensive rebounds).

Portland is perfectly capable of handling themselves in that arena, though, as a big team averaging just three rebounds less than the Spurs per game (which is, hilariously, the difference between 1st and 11th in rebounds).

One of the game's biggest questions will be the status of Victor Wembanyama, obviously, but De'Aaron Fox's status will also be a low-key point of interest (after being tripped by Jeremiah Fears against the Pelicans), as Portland's guard play is one of their most significant weaknesses.

Portland ranks in the bottom 20 in assists (22nd), turnovers given up (21st), turnover percentage (22nd), and assist-to-turnover ratio (22nd), all areas the Spurs rank inside the Top 5 in (3rd, 3rd, 3rd, and 1st).

If Wembanyama takes the court, this should be a win for the Spurs, barring another cold spell from three from San Antonio, or a scalding performance from Portland.

But even without Wembanyama, the Spurs might be able to wreak real havoc on the Trail Blazers' front-court with the presence of a healthy Fox.

Minus their availability, things might get a little sticky for the Spurs against a team deep enough for Jrue Holiday to be getting 6th Man of the Year consideration already. Though not if Stephon Castle has his say in the matter.

Here's hoping the Spurs get a little more heath luck than the bit they've already had.

Spurs Injuries: Victor Wembanyama: Questionable (Back), De'Aaron Fox: Questionable (Ankle), Tobias Harris: Out (Calf), Jayden Quaintance: Out (Knee), Dylan Harper: Out (Hip)

Trail Blazers Injuries: Ja Morant: Out (Leg), Shaedon Sharpe: Out (Knee), Chris Youngblood: Questionable (Heel), Vit Krejci: Out (Ankle)

#2876. Warriors feeling psychological boost of having injured Steph Curry around team

Warriors feeling psychological boost of having injured Steph Curry around team originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SAN FRANCISCO – Warriors guard Brandon Williams scrimmaged Tuesday and is expected to return Wednesday against the Sacramento Kings after missing the last six games because of a mild left hamstring strain. Williams might as well have been listed with an illness, too.

Williams was injured in fewer than two minutes against the New Orleans Pelicans on Nov. 9. Steph Curry, in only 21 minutes, scored 25 points on 6-of-10 shooting, 4-of-7 from 3-point range and a perfect 9-of-9 from the free-throw line in a 138-87 win. The Warriors' 51-point beatdown put the cherry on top of a 6-0 homestand that extended their win streak to eight straight games.

The Warriors' perfect homestand ended on a Monday. The next Monday, in a game without injured Williams and Curry, the Warriors lost by 21 points in New Orleans to a Pelicans team without Trey Murphy III.

Curry grabbed at his right side in the opening minutes of the Warriors' five-game road trip in an eventual seven-point loss against the Houston Rockets. Curry was ruled out the next day for the rest of the road trip with a right abdominal oblique strain. His two-week re-evaluation is expected within the coming days.

Golden State lost its next four games by a combined 109 points.

Williams traveled with the team and watched the offensive struggle from the bench. The same can be said for his view of the Warriors scoring 88 points in their fifth straight game below 100 points, yet found a way to beat the Boston Celtics by one on Sunday night.

"Yeah, I ain't gonna lie, s–t's been tough watching that," Williams said Tuesday at Chase Center after practice.

Watching the eyesore reminded Williams of why he's here, and why he has been so valued early this season.

"I know exactly what I need to do. Just watching from afar, you see a little bit more," Williams said. "The offense gets stagnant at times, and I know that's what they brought me in here for – to keep it rolling, keep it going, keep everybody organized and stuff. So hopefully we see that next time on the court for sure."

Even with Curry, Williams was going to be missed. Without the two-time NBA MVP, Williams' importance has been amplified as a playmaker who has a scorer's mindset and can make defenses react to him from beating them off the dribble.

Williams scored 20 points when Curry missed his first game to rest this season. He's the Warriors' fourth-leading scorer at 10.4 points per game, but ranks second in points per 36 minutes (23.3).

The Warriors will take any offensive help they can find until Curry is back, and Williams is sure to bring life to a unit that has looked listless as of late.

While Williams will be an instant boost to the Warriors, Curry already was one against the Celtics, despite his injury.

Curry was at Warriors shootaround in San Antonio after his injury but flew home the same day hours before the game and missed the massive FAA flight interruptions. He's the sun in the Warriors' solar system, as coach Steve Kerr often says, and that includes emotionally as well.

The Warriors ached for Curry's return right when he went down. His presence was sorely missed outside of box score stats during the rest of a historically bad road trip, and it was again felt rooting from the sidelines Sunday night as the underdog Warriors upset the Celtics.

"Definitely helpful," Kerr said Tuesday. "I mean, he's just always a breath of fresh air. And the humor and the confidence that he has, you saw it the other night. Draymond [Green] hits a three from the corner, he's flying all over the place on the bench.

"There's just a different vibe when Steph is in the building, and it's good to have him here, even if he's not playing."

Curry got out of his chair and turned to the Warriors' bench in celebration when Green, hours after going at it all day on social media with former Sixth Man of the Year and 17-year NBA vet Eddie Johnson, opened the game with a three from the right corner.

Curry threw three fingers up in unison with teammates when Green drained his second three of the first quarter, again letting it fly from the right corner.

And when Curry's longest-tenured teammate made his third three from the right corner in the first six minutes of the game, he almost melted into his chair and fell onto the court.

Sunday's win that snapped the Warriors' five-game losing streak started with Green making threes, and ended with him stopping Jayson Tatum's game-winning attempt. Green and Kerr shared an extended, emotional hug. Curry waited behind for Green and embraced him as the two enjoyed another countless moment of their competitive spirits intertwined.

Rookie Yaxel Lendeborg needed Curry in every way during the Warriors' skid. He needed Curry's gravity that made the start of his career so smooth. He also needed his calming presence that inspires confidence.

Curry wasn't there when Lendeborg gave everyone an inside view into his mind and said he's "mentally f–ked up" after the Warriors lost by 38 points against the Spurs the game after losing their superstar. He wasn't there as Lendeborg hung his head in New Orleans, or when he looked lost in Dallas and Memphis.

But Curry was there Sunday in San Francisco sharing knowledge with Lendeborg, who liked a post about the moment on Instagram.

"Yeah, it's huge," Kerr said about Lendeborg being around Curry. "And I've said this, but I love the internal leadership we have on this team. Our vets have been fantastic. Draymond's leadership every day has been brilliant. Al and Steph are more the quiet leaders by example, but pull a guy aside and talk to him.

"Our veteran players have provided such great leadership for us this year, and it's really helpful, especially for the young guys, Yaxel in particular, given how much he's playing."

This is all new to Williams. His prior teams, the Portland Trail Blazers and Dallas Mavericks, saw stars like Damian Lillard, Anthony Davis, Kyrie Irving and others miss time due to injuries. Curry is different, and so is the culture he has built for the Warriors.

Having him on the bench and around the team is personally helpful to Williams, and Curry's backup point guard is impressed by the Warriors' resolve in recovering from being hit in the mouth.

"It helps a lot, specifically for me," Williams said. "Those teams that I've been on, like, Kyrie went down, the morale around the team just went down. It's the complete opposite case here. Everybody's the next man up. I think the thing out here, what is it, Strength In Numbers? I think that's a great motto to live by, and it's perfect for us right now.

"Everybody's got to step up, whether it's Gui [Santos], Will [Richard], Graham [Ike], [Malevy Leons], all of us got to step up. It's a collective group effort."

Narratives have run rampant since the Warriors' 2022 championship season. The numbers still tell an obvious story. The Warriors have gone 39-53, a 42.4 win percentage, without Curry in the regular season the last five years. They're 2-5 this season, and 2-6 if you include their loss in Houston.

Curry was in attendance for the Warriors' first loss without him this season, a hard-fought nine-point defeat in Phoenix on the second night of a back-to-back after playing the night before in San Francisco. He went wild on the sidelines during his second rest game as Lendeborg unleashed 37 points in a home win over the LA Clippers and could hardly contain himself, bringing energy to the bench as the Warriors got back in the win column against the Celtics.

Greatness is contagious, even if it's felt in different ways than usual.
